People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… what finally pushed me to come in is I’ve been losing my place at work. Like, I’ll be scheduling appointments and suddenly I can’t remember where I was in the steps, or I’m putting together the lobby displays and my mind just sort of slips off. And then I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked.

It’s embarrassing because I’m usually pretty organized—well, I try to be, anyway. At the front desk there are so many little things going on, and even the magazine rack or the seasonal notices, people notice if something’s off. So I keep getting pulled into these thoughts, and then I lose track of what I was actually doing.

So I keep getting pulled into these thoughts, and then I lose track of what I was actually doing.\n\nDoctor: Are these memory slips and losing track of tasks causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going in and I get through the day, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning. But it does upset me. Scheduling appointments takes me longer now because I keep checking myself—like, “Did I put that in the right slot, did I tell them the right thing?” And then I lose time.\n\nAnd with the lobby displays—this sounds silly, but people really do notice those things—I’ve had to redo parts of them because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. So yes, it’s distressing, even if I’m technically still managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. I’m usually still aware I’m at the desk or in the lobby, and I know what I’m doing—or what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nIt’s more like my thoughts wander off and then I’m suddenly behind, if that makes sense. Like when you walk into the supply room for pens and then notice the forms are low, and then you remember the copier’s been making that line on the page… and then you’re standing there like, “Why did I come in here?” But I wouldn’t say I feel outside of myself or like things aren’t real. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just the slip itself, I think it’s what my mind does with it afterward. Like if I forget where I was in a schedule, suddenly I’m worrying I put someone in the wrong time slot, and then that they’ll show up angry, and then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd it’s not only work, really. I’ve been worrying about all kinds of normal stuff—bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes—like one small mix-up is going to become a much bigger problem. Even something minor can stick in my head way longer than it probably should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things exactly. I still do the appointments and the displays and whatever needs doing.

looping, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another until I’m checking details in my head over and over.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or feeling People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.

My sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.

I’m usually still aware I’m at the desk or in the lobby, and I know what I’m doing—or what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nIt’s more like my thoughts wander off and then I’m suddenly behind, if that makes sense. Like when you walk into the supply room for pens and then notice the forms are low, and then you remember the copier’s been making that line on the page… and then you’re standing there like, “Why did I come in here?” But I wouldn’t say I feel outside of myself or like things aren’t real. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just the slip itself, I think it’s what my mind does with it afterward. Like if I forget where I was in a schedule, suddenly I’m worrying I put someone in the wrong time slot, and then that they’ll show up angry, and then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd it’s not only work, really. I’ve been worrying about all kinds of normal stuff—bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes—like one small mix-up is going to become a much bigger problem. Even something minor can stick in my head way longer than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you've been avoiding because of these memory slips or worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things exactly. I still do the appointments and the displays and whatever needs doing.\n\nI might put something off for a bit if I’m already feeling like my head is scattered—like I’ll straighten papers or answer a simpler call first, which probably isn’t the most efficient. My husband says I make “warm-up tasks” for myself, which… maybe I do. He does that with yard work, though, so I don’t know if he has room to talk.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s anything major I’ve stopped doing because of it. More like I hesitate, double-check, and take longer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have days where I feel worn down or kind of frustrated with myself, but “sad” feels like… I don’t know, heavier than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I get in a mood if the whole day has been one thing after another—like the phones at work won’t stop, or I get home and realize I forgot something from the store even though it was the one thing I went in for. Then I’ll be quiet for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and calm your worries or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.\n\nMy sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, probably yes. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but it’ll just keep circling around anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to focus on one thing, and then it turns into, “Did I check that detail? Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So yes, it’s distressing, even if I’m technically still managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. I’m usually still aware I’m at the desk or in the lobby, and I know what I’m doing—or what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nIt’s more like my thoughts wander off and then I’m suddenly behind, if that makes sense. Like when you walk into the supply room for pens and then notice the forms are low, and then you remember the copier’s been making that line on the page… and then you’re standing there like, “Why did I come in here?” But I wouldn’t say I feel outside of myself or like things aren’t real. I’ve been worrying about all kinds of normal stuff—bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes—like one small mix-up is going to become a much bigger problem. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. So yes, it’s distressing, even if I’m technically still managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. I’m usually still aware I’m at the desk or in the lobby, and I know what I’m doing—or what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nIt’s more like my thoughts wander off and then I’m suddenly behind, if that makes sense. Like when you walk into the supply room for pens and then notice the forms are low, and then you remember the copier’s been making that line on the page… and then you’re standing there like, “Why did I come in here?” But I wouldn’t say I feel outside of myself or like things aren’t real. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just the slip itself, I think it’s what my mind does with it afterward. Like if I forget where I was in a schedule, suddenly I’m worrying I put someone in the wrong time slot, and then that they’ll show up angry, and then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd it’s not only work, really. I’ve been worrying about all kinds of normal stuff—bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes—like one small mix-up is going to become a much bigger problem. Even something minor can stick in my head way longer than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you've been avoiding because of these memory slips or worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things exactly. I still do the appointments and the displays and whatever needs doing.\n\nI might put something off for a bit if I’m already feeling like my head is scattered—like I’ll straighten papers or answer a simpler call first, which probably isn’t the most efficient. My husband says I make “warm-up tasks” for myself, which… maybe I do. He does that with yard work, though, so I don’t know if he has room to talk.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s anything major I’ve stopped doing because of it. More like I hesitate, double-check, and take longer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have days where I feel worn down or kind of frustrated with myself, but “sad” feels like… I don’t know, heavier than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I get in a mood if the whole day has been one thing after another—like the phones at work won’t stop, or I get home and realize I forgot something from the store even though it was the one thing I went in for. Then I’ll be quiet for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and calm your worries or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.\n\nMy sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, probably yes. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but it’ll just keep circling around anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to focus on one thing, and then it turns into, “Did I check that detail? Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. Before that I’d get worried like anyone, but the memory slips and the second-guessing got more noticeable after that whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I think about it sometimes, because it kind of comes up when something reminds me of it, like paperwork or calls or things being filed wrong… and then I start thinking about all these little details.\n\nBut when you say “when I’m not trying to,” I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like my mind wanders there and then wanders off to something else. I’m sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it very directly. I haven’t really tracked how often it happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

He does that with yard work, though, so I don’t know if he has room to talk.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s anything major I’ve stopped doing because of it. More like I hesitate, double-check, and take longer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have days where I feel worn down or kind of frustrated with myself, but “sad” feels like… I don’t know, heavier than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I get in a mood if the whole day has been one thing after another—like the phones at work won’t stop, or I get home and realize I forgot something from the store even though it was the one thing I went in for. Then I’ll be quiet for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and calm your worries or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.\n\nMy sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, probably yes. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but it’ll just keep circling around anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to focus on one thing, and then it turns into, “Did I check that detail? Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. Before that I’d get worried like anyone, but the memory slips and the second-guessing got more noticeable after that whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I think about it sometimes, because it kind of comes up when something reminds me of it, like paperwork or calls or things being filed wrong… and then I start thinking about all these little details.\n\nBut when you say “when I’m not trying to,” I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like my mind wanders there and then wanders off to something else. I’m sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it very directly. I haven’t really tracked how often it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or anxious when you think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say fearful exactly. Anxious… maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s about that incident itself or more about everything getting tangled up around it, like who had the paperwork and whether something was missed.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about it and then I’m suddenly thinking about totally different forms at work, like if I told someone the wrong extension or forgot to write down a message, and then I’m not even on the detention thing anymore. So… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I haven’t really noticed a specific fear feeling every time I think about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally pushed me to come in is I’ve been losing my place at work. Like, I’ll be scheduling appointments and suddenly I can’t remember where I was in the steps, or I’m putting together the lobby displays and my mind just sort of slips off. And then I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m usually pretty organized—well, I try to be, anyway. At the front desk there are so many little things going on, and even the magazine rack or the seasonal notices, people notice if something’s off. So I keep getting pulled into these thoughts, and then I lose track of what I was actually doing.\n\nDoctor: Are these memory slips and losing track of tasks causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going in and I get through the day, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning. But it does upset me. Scheduling appointments takes me longer now because I keep checking myself—like, “Did I put that in the right slot, did I tell them the right thing?” And then I lose time.\n\nAnd with the lobby displays—this sounds silly, but people really do notice those things—I’ve had to redo parts of them because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. So yes, it’s distressing, even if I’m technically still managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. I’m usually still aware I’m at the desk or in the lobby, and I know what I’m doing—or what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nIt’s more like my thoughts wander off and then I’m suddenly behind, if that makes sense. Like when you walk into the supply room for pens and then notice the forms are low, and then you remember the copier’s been making that line on the page… and then you’re standing there like, “Why did I come in here?” But I wouldn’t say I feel outside of myself or like things aren’t real. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just the slip itself, I think it’s what my mind does with it afterward. Then I’ll be quiet for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and calm your worries or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.\n\nMy sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, probably yes. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but it’ll just keep circling around anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to focus on one thing, and then it turns into, “Did I check that detail? Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. Before that I’d get worried like anyone, but the memory slips and the second-guessing got more noticeable after that whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I think about it sometimes, because it kind of comes up when something reminds me of it, like paperwork or calls or things being filed wrong… and then I start thinking about all these little details.\n\nBut when you say “when I’m not trying to,” I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like my mind wanders there and then wanders off to something else. I’m sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it very directly. I haven’t really tracked how often it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or anxious when you think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say fearful exactly. Anxious… maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s about that incident itself or more about everything getting tangled up around it, like who had the paperwork and whether something was missed.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about it and then I’m suddenly thinking about totally different forms at work, like if I told someone the wrong extension or forgot to write down a message, and then I’m not even on the detention thing anymore. So… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I haven’t really noticed a specific fear feeling every time I think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about or talking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t bring it up much, but I also don’t know if that’s me avoiding it or just not wanting to get into a long explanation, because it always turns into this whole thing with details and dates and who said what.\n\nLike at work, if someone asks me something simple, I already worry I’m overexplaining, and then with this kind of thing it’s worse. So… maybe I sidestep it sometimes? But I haven’t really noticed myself deliberately trying not to think about it. It’s more that I get tired of going over it, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally pushed me to come in is I’ve been losing my place at work. Like, I’ll be scheduling appointments and suddenly I can’t remember where I was in the steps, or I’m putting together the lobby displays and my mind just sort of slips off. And then I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m usually pretty organized—well, I try to be, anyway. At the front desk there are so many little things going on, and even the magazine rack or the seasonal notices, people notice if something’s off. So I keep getting pulled into these thoughts, and then I lose track of what I was actually doing.\n\nDoctor: Are these memory slips and losing track of tasks causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going in and I get through the day, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning. But it does upset me. Scheduling appointments takes me longer now because I keep checking myself—like, “Did I put that in the right slot, did I tell them the right thing?” And then I lose time.\n\nAnd with the lobby displays—this sounds silly, but people really do notice those things—I’ve had to redo parts of them because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. So yes, it’s distressing, even if I’m technically still managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. I still do the appointments and the displays and whatever needs doing.\n\nI might put something off for a bit if I’m already feeling like my head is scattered—like I’ll straighten papers or answer a simpler call first, which probably isn’t the most efficient. My husband says I make “warm-up tasks” for myself, which… maybe I do. He does that with yard work, though, so I don’t know if he has room to talk.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s anything major I’ve stopped doing because of it. More like I hesitate, double-check, and take longer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have days where I feel worn down or kind of frustrated with myself, but “sad” feels like… I don’t know, heavier than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I get in a mood if the whole day has been one thing after another—like the phones at work won’t stop, or I get home and realize I forgot something from the store even though it was the one thing I went in for. Then I’ll be quiet for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and calm your worries or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.\n\nMy sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, probably yes. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but it’ll just keep circling around anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to focus on one thing, and then it turns into, “Did I check that detail? Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. Before that I’d get worried like anyone, but the memory slips and the second-guessing got more noticeable after that whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I think about it sometimes, because it kind of comes up when something reminds me of it, like paperwork or calls or things being filed wrong… and then I start thinking about all these little details.\n\nBut when you say “when I’m not trying to,” I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like my mind wanders there and then wanders off to something else. I’m sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it very directly. I haven’t really tracked how often it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or anxious when you think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say fearful exactly. Anxious… maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s about that incident itself or more about everything getting tangled up around it, like who had the paperwork and whether something was missed.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about it and then I’m suddenly thinking about totally different forms at work, like if I told someone the wrong extension or forgot to write down a message, and then I’m not even on the detention thing anymore. So… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I haven’t really noticed a specific fear feeling every time I think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about or talking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t bring it up much, but I also don’t know if that’s me avoiding it or just not wanting to get into a long explanation, because it always turns into this whole thing with details and dates and who said what.\n\nLike at work, if someone asks me something simple, I already worry I’m overexplaining, and then with this kind of thing it’s worse. So… maybe I sidestep it sometimes? But I haven’t really noticed myself deliberately trying not to think about it. It’s more that I get tired of going over it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself deliberately trying not to think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I sit there and deliberately push it away, no. Or… I’m not sure. If it pops into my head, I might kind of move on to something else, but that’s also what I do with a lot of things, like if I remember I forgot to call someone back or I didn’t put a note in the right place.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as trying not to think about it. I haven’t really caught myself saying, “Don’t think about that.” It’s more like my brain gets busy with the next thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Scheduling appointments takes me longer now because I keep checking myself—like, “Did I put that in the right slot, did I tell them the right thing?” And then I lose time.\n\nAnd with the lobby displays—this sounds silly, but people really do notice those things—I’ve had to redo parts of them because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. So yes, it’s distressing, even if I’m technically still managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. I’m usually still aware I’m at the desk or in the lobby, and I know what I’m doing—or what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nIt’s more like my thoughts wander off and then I’m suddenly behind, if that makes sense. Like when you walk into the supply room for pens and then notice the forms are low, and then you remember the copier’s been making that line on the page… and then you’re standing there like, “Why did I come in here?” But I wouldn’t say I feel outside of myself or like things aren’t real. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just the slip itself, I think it’s what my mind does with it afterward. Like if I forget where I was in a schedule, suddenly I’m worrying I put someone in the wrong time slot, and then that they’ll show up angry, and then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd it’s not only work, really. I’ve been worrying about all kinds of normal stuff—bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes—like one small mix-up is going to become a much bigger problem. Even something minor can stick in my head way longer than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you've been avoiding because of these memory slips or worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things exactly. I still do the appointments and the displays and whatever needs doing.\n\nI might put something off for a bit if I’m already feeling like my head is scattered—like I’ll straighten papers or answer a simpler call first, which probably isn’t the most efficient. My husband says I make “warm-up tasks” for myself, which… maybe I do. He does that with yard work, though, so I don’t know if he has room to talk.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s anything major I’ve stopped doing because of it. More like I hesitate, double-check, and take longer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have days where I feel worn down or kind of frustrated with myself, but “sad” feels like… I don’t know, heavier than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I get in a mood if the whole day has been one thing after another—like the phones at work won’t stop, or I get home and realize I forgot something from the store even though it was the one thing I went in for. Then I’ll be quiet for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and calm your worries or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.\n\nMy sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, probably yes. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but it’ll just keep circling around anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to focus on one thing, and then it turns into, “Did I check that detail? Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. Before that I’d get worried like anyone, but the memory slips and the second-guessing got more noticeable after that whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I think about it sometimes, because it kind of comes up when something reminds me of it, like paperwork or calls or things being filed wrong… and then I start thinking about all these little details.\n\nBut when you say “when I’m not trying to,” I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like my mind wanders there and then wanders off to something else. I’m sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it very directly. I haven’t really tracked how often it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or anxious when you think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say fearful exactly. Anxious… maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s about that incident itself or more about everything getting tangled up around it, like who had the paperwork and whether something was missed.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about it and then I’m suddenly thinking about totally different forms at work, like if I told someone the wrong extension or forgot to write down a message, and then I’m not even on the detention thing anymore. So… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I haven’t really noticed a specific fear feeling every time I think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about or talking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t bring it up much, but I also don’t know if that’s me avoiding it or just not wanting to get into a long explanation, because it always turns into this whole thing with details and dates and who said what.\n\nLike at work, if someone asks me something simple, I already worry I’m overexplaining, and then with this kind of thing it’s worse. So… maybe I sidestep it sometimes? But I haven’t really noticed myself deliberately trying not to think about it. It’s more that I get tired of going over it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself deliberately trying not to think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I sit there and deliberately push it away, no. Or… I’m not sure. If it pops into my head, I might kind of move on to something else, but that’s also what I do with a lot of things, like if I remember I forgot to call someone back or I didn’t put a note in the right place.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as trying not to think about it. I haven’t really caught myself saying, “Don’t think about that.” It’s more like my brain gets busy with the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places, people, or activities that remind you of the overnight detention incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I avoid specific places or people because of it, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, there are places I don’t like going just because parking is terrible or because I run into people from work and then I feel like I have to make small talk, but I don’t know if that’s connected to the detention thing. Activities… no, I can’t think of anything I’ve stopped doing because it reminds me of that. If there is something, it’s not obvious to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. Before that I’d get worried like anyone, but the memory slips and the second-guessing got more noticeable after that whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I think about it sometimes, because it kind of comes up when something reminds me of it, like paperwork or calls or things being filed wrong… and then I start thinking about all these little details.\n\nBut when you say “when I’m not trying to,” I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like my mind wanders there and then wanders off to something else. I’m sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it very directly. I haven’t really tracked how often it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or anxious when you think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say fearful exactly. Anxious… maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s about that incident itself or more about everything getting tangled up around it, like who had the paperwork and whether something was missed.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about it and then I’m suddenly thinking about totally different forms at work, like if I told someone the wrong extension or forgot to write down a message, and then I’m not even on the detention thing anymore. So… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I haven’t really noticed a specific fear feeling every time I think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about or talking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t bring it up much, but I also don’t know if that’s me avoiding it or just not wanting to get into a long explanation, because it always turns into this whole thing with details and dates and who said what.\n\nLike at work, if someone asks me something simple, I already worry I’m overexplaining, and then with this kind of thing it’s worse. So… maybe I sidestep it sometimes? But I haven’t really noticed myself deliberately trying not to think about it. It’s more that I get tired of going over it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself deliberately trying not to think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I sit there and deliberately push it away, no. Or… I’m not sure. If it pops into my head, I might kind of move on to something else, but that’s also what I do with a lot of things, like if I remember I forgot to call someone back or I didn’t put a note in the right place.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as trying not to think about it. I haven’t really caught myself saying, “Don’t think about that.” It’s more like my brain gets busy with the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places, people, or activities that remind you of the overnight detention incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I avoid specific places or people because of it, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, there are places I don’t like going just because parking is terrible or because I run into people from work and then I feel like I have to make small talk, but I don’t know if that’s connected to the detention thing. Activities… no, I can’t think of anything I’ve stopped doing because it reminds me of that. If there is something, it’s not obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts about the overnight detention incident that come into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, random thoughts come into my head all the time, like if I’m at the front desk and I suddenly remember I didn’t send a fax or I need to check whether someone signed in properly, and then my mind jumps somewhere else.\n\nWith that incident… I can’t really say it pops in on its own in a big way. Maybe sometimes something vaguely reminds me of it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as a memory coming in without me meaning to think about it. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're avoiding talking about the details of the overnight detention incident with others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m avoiding it exactly. I mean, I also don’t really go around talking about personal things in detail with people anyway, even normal stuff. Like at work, if someone asks how my weekend was, I just say “fine,” because otherwise it turns into a whole conversation and then the phone rings and I lose track of what I was doing.\n\nSo with that incident… I guess I haven’t really talked through all the details with people, but I’m not sure if that’s avoidance or just me not wanting to get into a long thing. I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I can’t talk about this.” It’s more like… it doesn’t come up much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally pushed me to come in is I’ve been losing my place at work. Like, I’ll be scheduling appointments and suddenly I can’t remember where I was in the steps, or I’m putting together the lobby displays and my mind just sort of slips off. And then I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m usually pretty organized—well, I try to be, anyway. At the front desk there are so many little things going on, and even the magazine rack or the seasonal notices, people notice if something’s off. So I keep getting pulled into these thoughts, and then I lose track of what I was actually doing.\n\nDoctor: Are these memory slips and losing track of tasks causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going in and I get through the day, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning. But it does upset me. Scheduling appointments takes me longer now because I keep checking myself—like, “Did I put that in the right slot, did I tell them the right thing?” And then I lose time.\n\nAnd with the lobby displays—this sounds silly, but people really do notice those things—I’ve had to redo parts of them because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. So yes, it’s distressing, even if I’m technically still managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. I’m usually still aware I’m at the desk or in the lobby, and I know what I’m doing—or what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nIt’s more like my thoughts wander off and then I’m suddenly behind, if that makes sense. Like when you walk into the supply room for pens and then notice the forms are low, and then you remember the copier’s been making that line on the page… and then you’re standing there like, “Why did I come in here?” But I wouldn’t say I feel outside of myself or like things aren’t real. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just the slip itself, I think it’s what my mind does with it afterward. Like if I forget where I was in a schedule, suddenly I’m worrying I put someone in the wrong time slot, and then that they’ll show up angry, and then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd it’s not only work, really. I’ve been worrying about all kinds of normal stuff—bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes—like one small mix-up is going to become a much bigger problem. Even something minor can stick in my head way longer than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you've been avoiding because of these memory slips or worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things exactly. I still do the appointments and the displays and whatever needs doing.\n\nI might put something off for a bit if I’m already feeling like my head is scattered—like I’ll straighten papers or answer a simpler call first, which probably isn’t the most efficient. My husband says I make “warm-up tasks” for myself, which… maybe I do. He does that with yard work, though, so I don’t know if he has room to talk.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s anything major I’ve stopped doing because of it. More like I hesitate, double-check, and take longer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have days where I feel worn down or kind of frustrated with myself, but “sad” feels like… I don’t know, heavier than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I get in a mood if the whole day has been one thing after another—like the phones at work won’t stop, or I get home and realize I forgot something from the store even though it was the one thing I went in for. Then I’ll be quiet for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and calm your worries or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.\n\nMy sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, probably yes. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but it’ll just keep circling around anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to focus on one thing, and then it turns into, “Did I check that detail? Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. Before that I’d get worried like anyone, but the memory slips and the second-guessing got more noticeable after that whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I think about it sometimes, because it kind of comes up when something reminds me of it, like paperwork or calls or things being filed wrong… and then I start thinking about all these little details.\n\nBut when you say “when I’m not trying to,” I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like my mind wanders there and then wanders off to something else. I’m sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it very directly. I haven’t really tracked how often it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or anxious when you think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say fearful exactly. Anxious… maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s about that incident itself or more about everything getting tangled up around it, like who had the paperwork and whether something was missed.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about it and then I’m suddenly thinking about totally different forms at work, like if I told someone the wrong extension or forgot to write down a message, and then I’m not even on the detention thing anymore. So… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I haven’t really noticed a specific fear feeling every time I think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about or talking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t bring it up much, but I also don’t know if that’s me avoiding it or just not wanting to get into a long explanation, because it always turns into this whole thing with details and dates and who said what.\n\nLike at work, if someone asks me something simple, I already worry I’m overexplaining, and then with this kind of thing it’s worse. So… maybe I sidestep it sometimes? But I haven’t really noticed myself deliberately trying not to think about it. It’s more that I get tired of going over it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself deliberately trying not to think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I sit there and deliberately push it away, no. Or… I’m not sure. If it pops into my head, I might kind of move on to something else, but that’s also what I do with a lot of things, like if I remember I forgot to call someone back or I didn’t put a note in the right place.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as trying not to think about it. I haven’t really caught myself saying, “Don’t think about that.” It’s more like my brain gets busy with the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places, people, or activities that remind you of the overnight detention incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I avoid specific places or people because of it, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, there are places I don’t like going just because parking is terrible or because I run into people from work and then I feel like I have to make small talk, but I don’t know if that’s connected to the detention thing. Activities… no, I can’t think of anything I’ve stopped doing because it reminds me of that. If there is something, it’s not obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts about the overnight detention incident that come into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, random thoughts come into my head all the time, like if I’m at the front desk and I suddenly remember I didn’t send a fax or I need to check whether someone signed in properly, and then my mind jumps somewhere else.\n\nWith that incident… I can’t really say it pops in on its own in a big way. Maybe sometimes something vaguely reminds me of it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as a memory coming in without me meaning to think about it. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're avoiding talking about the details of the overnight detention incident with others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m avoiding it exactly. I mean, I also don’t really go around talking about personal things in detail with people anyway, even normal stuff. Like at work, if someone asks how my weekend was, I just say “fine,” because otherwise it turns into a whole conversation and then the phone rings and I lose track of what I was doing.\n\nSo with that incident… I guess I haven’t really talked through all the details with people, but I’m not sure if that’s avoidance or just me not wanting to get into a long thing. I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I can’t talk about this.” It’s more like… it doesn’t come up much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts about the overnight detention incident that feel like they're intruding into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying, like it barges in while I’m trying to focus. My mind wanders a lot anyway, especially at work—like I’ll be typing something and then remember the copy machine needs paper, or that someone left their badge at the desk, and then I’m thinking about three other things.\n\nWith the overnight detention thing, I guess I might think of it if something reminds me, but I can’t really say it intrudes. I haven’t noticed it taking over when I’m trying to think about something else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t think it’s really hurt my relationships in a big way. I’m still showing up, you know, still getting through the day. With family or friends, I might be a little distracted, but I can’t say there’s been some major falling-out because of it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally pushed me to come in is I’ve been losing my place at work. And then I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m usually pretty organized—well, I try to be, anyway. At the front desk there are so many little things going on, and even the magazine rack or the seasonal notices, people notice if something’s off. So I keep getting pulled into these thoughts, and then I lose track of what I was actually doing.\n\nDoctor: Are these memory slips and losing track of tasks causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going in and I get through the day, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning. But it does upset me. Scheduling appointments takes me longer now because I keep checking myself—like, “Did I put that in the right slot, did I tell them the right thing?” And then I lose time.\n\nAnd with the lobby displays—this sounds silly, but people really do notice those things—I’ve had to redo parts of them because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. So yes, it’s distressing, even if I’m technically still managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. I’m usually still aware I’m at the desk or in the lobby, and I know what I’m doing—or what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nIt’s more like my thoughts wander off and then I’m suddenly behind, if that makes sense. Like when you walk into the supply room for pens and then notice the forms are low, and then you remember the copier’s been making that line on the page… and then you’re standing there like, “Why did I come in here?” But I wouldn’t say I feel outside of myself or like things aren’t real. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these memory slips happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just the slip itself, I think it’s what my mind does with it afterward. Like if I forget where I was in a schedule, suddenly I’m worrying I put someone in the wrong time slot, and then that they’ll show up angry, and then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd it’s not only work, really. I’ve been worrying about all kinds of normal stuff—bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes—like one small mix-up is going to become a much bigger problem. Even something minor can stick in my head way longer than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you've been avoiding because of these memory slips or worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things exactly. I still do the appointments and the displays and whatever needs doing.\n\nI might put something off for a bit if I’m already feeling like my head is scattered—like I’ll straighten papers or answer a simpler call first, which probably isn’t the most efficient. My husband says I make “warm-up tasks” for myself, which… maybe I do. He does that with yard work, though, so I don’t know if he has room to talk.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s anything major I’ve stopped doing because of it. More like I hesitate, double-check, and take longer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have days where I feel worn down or kind of frustrated with myself, but “sad” feels like… I don’t know, heavier than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I get in a mood if the whole day has been one thing after another—like the phones at work won’t stop, or I get home and realize I forgot something from the store even though it was the one thing I went in for. Then I’ll be quiet for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and calm your worries or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do check things more than once sometimes, like looking back at a calendar or making sure I wrote a note down, but that feels more like trying not to make a dumb mistake than, you know, some rule I have to follow.\n\nMy sister used to do this thing where she had to tap the stove knobs before leaving the house, and I don’t really have anything like that. I’ll just get nervous and look something over again, then maybe get sidetracked by another thing I notice needs fixing. So… no, I haven’t noticed repeating actions to stop something bad from happening, at least not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, probably yes. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but it’ll just keep circling around anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to focus on one thing, and then it turns into, “Did I check that detail? Did I write that down right?” and then somehow I’m on to the next concern, and the next. It’s not like I want to sit there replaying it, but my mind sort of keeps going back over the details. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been checking it in my head for a while instead of just moving on.\n\nDoctor: Was there a particular event or situation that you think might have triggered these memory slips and worries for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it started around nine months ago, give or take. It was after this overnight detention thing, and then there was a paperwork mix-up around it, which… I still hate even saying because it sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about who filed what and who was supposed to call who.\n\nBut that’s when I noticed it, I think. Before that I’d get worried like anyone, but the memory slips and the second-guessing got more noticeable after that whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I think about it sometimes, because it kind of comes up when something reminds me of it, like paperwork or calls or things being filed wrong… and then I start thinking about all these little details.\n\nBut when you say “when I’m not trying to,” I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like my mind wanders there and then wanders off to something else. I’m sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it very directly. I haven’t really tracked how often it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or anxious when you think about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say fearful exactly. Anxious… maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s about that incident itself or more about everything getting tangled up around it, like who had the paperwork and whether something was missed.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about it and then I’m suddenly thinking about totally different forms at work, like if I told someone the wrong extension or forgot to write down a message, and then I’m not even on the detention thing anymore. So… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I haven’t really noticed a specific fear feeling every time I think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about or talking about the overnight detention incident from nine months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t bring it up much, but I also don’t know if that’s me avoiding it or just not wanting to get into a long explanation, because it always turns into this whole thing with details and dates and who said what.\n\nLike at work, if someone asks me something simple, I already worry I’m overexplaining, and then with this kind of thing it’s worse. I haven’t really caught myself saying, “Don’t think about that.” It’s more like my brain gets busy with the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places, people, or activities that remind you of the overnight detention incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I avoid specific places or people because of it, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, there are places I don’t like going just because parking is terrible or because I run into people from work and then I feel like I have to make small talk, but I don’t know if that’s connected to the detention thing. Activities… no, I can’t think of anything I’ve stopped doing because it reminds me of that. If there is something, it’s not obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts about the overnight detention incident that come into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure.
